# Side issues: data, labels and transcription

Found while surveying the DB dump (the read-only DB dump of 2026-09-26) against the question bank (`lsatu-wt-parser/public/data`). Companion to [[revamp-list]] and [[choice-note-issues]]. Ordered by how much a student could be misled.

## 1. Content that belongs to a different question

These show a student reasoning that does not match the question in front of them.

| Question | What is wrong | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| PT108-S3-Q6 | The DB stimulus and all five notes are for a different question from the official one. | DB stimulus: the bass catch downstream of the power plant fell. Bank stimulus for PT108-S3-Q6: the algae population downstream *rose*, with choices about detergents, water temperature and dissolved oxygen. The notes talk about predators, anglers and cooler water, which match neither set of choices. DB key C and bank key C agree only by coincidence. Needs a source check before anything else. |
| PT137-S3-Q25 | Evaluate step is from another question. | "Denise and Reshmi agree that people decide whether to commit a crime." The speakers are Graham and Adelaide (chess computer). |
| PT143-S4-Q18 | Speaker table and Evaluate are from PT137-S3-Q25's template. | Table headed "Graham / Adelaide" (speakers are Wong and Tate); Evaluate says they disagree about countries being "better off as democracies" (the issue is autocracies). |
| PT135-S4-Q25 | A note is from another question. | "Neither Samuel nor Tova address a general trend of modern life." Speakers are Lutsina and Priscilla. |
| PT133-S3-Q4 | E note carries speakers from PT142-S2-Q7. | "Neither Talbert nor Sklar would agree with this statement." Speakers are Sam and Meli. |

## 2. Suspected mis-keys

No explanation argues for a letter other than `correct_choice`. The scan's 16 "key conflict" hits in notes were all "Yes, the author assumes this, but..." openings. Two related items:

- **PT158-S4-Q22** (Weaken, d5): the breakdown's Goal predicts the "different epistemologists contradict each other" objection, which is choice D. The key (E, "much debate as to which thinkers were epistemologists") is right, and the E note explains it well, but the breakdown steers students to D and the D note then calls contradiction "a feature, not a bug". Rewrite the breakdown.
- **Bank answer keys disagree with the DB on 18 questions**, all of them bank entries filed under a different PT, section or question number ("moved" in `revamp-inventory.csv` (in this folder)). I read five of these (PT116-S2-Q6, Q7, Q8; PT131-S3-Q16, Q17) and the DB key is correct in every case. So the bank's `correctAnswer` is wrong for these, not the DB. Anything that reads the bank's key (drills, analytics, the golden-survey inventory) would mis-grade them:

  PT101-S2-Q19 (DB E, bank D), PT101-S3-Q19 (D/E), PT113-S3-Q21 (C/A), PT113-S3-Q22 (D/A), PT113-S3-Q23 (B/A), PT113-S3-Q24 (E/A), PT116-S2-Q4 (B/C), PT116-S2-Q6 (E/B), PT116-S2-Q7 (C/D), PT116-S2-Q8 (C/E), PT119-S2-Q19 (B/E), PT119-S2-Q21 (A/C), PT119-S4-Q8 (A/C), PT129-S3-Q18 (B/D), PT129-S3-Q19 (D/A), PT131-S3-Q16 (E/C), PT131-S3-Q17 (A/E), PT148-S4-Q24 (E/A).

  Note PT113-S3-Q21 to Q24 all have bank key A, which looks like a placeholder.

## 3. Bank alignment

- **PT119-S2-Q19** is matched to bank entry PT103-S2-Q24, which is a different question on the same topic (musical intervals; the bank one is about an isolated tribe's preferences). Its difficulty and features in `revamp-inventory.csv` (in this folder) are borrowed from the wrong question.
- **PT101-S3-Q1**: the bank stimulus differs from the DB's in both speakers' text (bank: election boards hiring extra help; Sharon concludes "apathy"). The DB text reads like the real question. The bank's choices for this question may not match either.
- 110 explained questions have no bank entry at all, so there is no difficulty and no answer-choice text on disk to check notes against (for example PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S3-Q3, PT131-S2-Q5).

## 4. Empty or partial explanations

| Problem | Count | Questions |
|---|---|---|
| Explanation row exists with no steps and no notes | 2 | PT122-S1-Q9, PT147-S4-Q6 |
| Breakdown only, no choice notes | 16 | PT102-S3-Q14, PT117-S3-Q2, PT117-S3-Q8, PT117-S4-Q3, PT117-S4-Q5, PT118-S1-Q9, PT118-S3-Q8, PT119-S2-Q7, PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S2-Q24, PT119-S3-Q3, PT119-S3-Q20, PT119-S4-Q2, PT120-S1-Q23, PT120-S1-Q26, PT120-S4-Q17 |
| Some notes missing | 8 | PT105-S4-Q14 (3 of 5), PT133-S2-Q25 (3, including the correct answer), PT107-S3-Q7, PT108-S3-Q16, PT111-S1-Q22, PT119-S2-Q15, PT146-S3-Q11, PT156-S2-Q19 (4 each) |
| Placeholder text as a note | 2 | PT147-S4-Q4 A (correct), PT147-S4-Q3 D (correct): "(No authored tag or explanation for this choice.)" |
| Notes but no Argument Breakdown | 224 | Heaviest in PT148 (33), PT111 (14), PT122 (11), PT116 (10), PT102 (9). |
| Question has no explanation at all | 352 | Known (`is_explained = false`); the 772-gap plan covers these. |

Several breakdown-only rows (PT117-S3-Q8, PT118-S1-Q9, PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S3-Q3, PT119-S2-Q24, PT119-S4-Q2, PT120-S1-Q23, PT120-S4-Q17) are written in a different register from the LSAT Lab text: bracketed labels like "Rossi's argument structure", em-dashes, "i.e." glosses. They look like later fill-ins written without the answer choices. Two of them (PT119-S2-Q12, PT119-S3-Q3) have no bank entry, so the choices may never have been available to whoever wrote them.

- "click on the bold if you'd like to see other examples": PT140-S3-Q7.

## 7. Question-type labels

`lr_questions.question_type` has **82 distinct values** for about 18 real types: the same type appears as "Must be True", "Must Be True", "Must Be True (Inference)", "Must be True (EXCEPT)"; "Paradox EXCEPT" and "Paradox (EXCEPT)"; 30-odd "(type inferred)" suffixes. Anything that groups by this column (drill filters, analytics) will split types.

Labels that are plainly wrong for the stem:

# Choice Note problems confirmed by reading

Each row was checked against the stimulus and the answer choices in the question bank (`lsatu-wt-parser/public/data`), since the DB rows do not store the choices. Companion to [[revamp-list]].

**Key conflicts:** none found. The scan flagged 16 notes that seemed to call a wrong letter correct; every one was a "Yes, the author assumes this, but..." opening. No explanation argues for a letter other than `correct_choice`. Two items point students the wrong way without contradicting the key: PT158-S4-Q22 (the breakdown predicts wrong choice D) and PT108-S3-Q6 (the notes belong to a different question; see side-issues).

**Popular traps:** the DB has no answer-choice percentages, and the local markdown that does was out of scope, so "verdict-only note on the popular trap" could not be tested directly. What can be said: 577 explanations have at least one wrong-answer note under 15 words or a bare "not relevant" / "out of scope" verdict (`verdict_only` column in `revamp-inventory.csv` (in this folder)), and in the 100 Weak items that is the typical note.

## Misquotes, and notes written for a different question (9)

| Question | Type | Diff | Choice | Problem | Quote or detail |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| PT105-S4-Q14 | Necessary Assumption | 4 | D (correct) | misquote | "Choice (D) states that upper-income citizens could use the money"; D says "would use". |
| PT108-S3-Q6 | Paradox | 4 | all | wrong question | All five notes (predators, anglers, cooler water) answer a different question from the official one (algae increase; choices about detergents, temperature, oxygen). |
| PT137-S3-Q25 | Agree/Disagree | 2 | breakdown | wrong question | Evaluate: "Denise and Reshmi agree that people decide whether to commit a crime." |
| PT137-S4-Q15 | MBT/Most Supported | 4 | B | misquote | Note: "will worsen soil damage"; choice says "worsen soil drainage". |
| PT158-S2-Q22 | Parallel Flaw | 4 | D | misquote | Describes D as "not in the town's best interest to use the land for recreation"; D is about developing the other borders. |
| PT158-S3-Q2 | Strengthen | 1 | D | misquote | Note is about students who "can effectively combine knowledge"; D says they "can effectively solve many problems". (Also found in the 2026-09-26 survey.) |
| PT158-S3-Q9 | Weaken | 4 | C | misquote | Treats "negligible price increases for consumers" as "negligible additional costs to drug companies". |
| PT158-S3-Q13 | Necessary Assumption | 5 | E | misquote | Argues against "will not help patients address any difficulties they have in their relationships", not in E. |
| PT158-S3-Q17 | Principle-Conform | 5 | A, B, C, D | misquote | Notes describe choices that do not exist: "(2) enjoy learning math", "(2) have customers recommend the product", "(2) borrowers to return books in good condition", "(2) its employees to start working on Saturdays". |

  A: Anyone who has no time for leisure activities works at least 18 hours a day.  [Compatible]
  B: Some entrepreneurs who work at least 18 hours a day are successful.  [Compatible]
  C: Some happy entrepreneurs are successful.  [Compatible]
  D: Some entrepreneurs who work at least 18 hours a day are happy.  [Correct]
  E: Some successful entrepreneurs work less than 18 hours a day.  [Compatible]

  STEP 0 [Statements] fb5db01f-df99-434b-bc1a-844cf97080bd
*Quantifiers / Conditionals* (most, no, all)

Any time we have a mix of quantified claims and conditionals, we want to prioritize conditionals.

Happy → Time for
entrepreneur Leisure

Work at → No time for
least 18hrs Leisure

These both have the concept of "time for leisure", so we should see whether they chain together. If we contrapose either one, we can chain them together.

Happy → Time for → Work less than
entrepreneur Leisure 18 hrs / day

Then we get our *Most* claim.

*Most* successful *are* Work at least
entrepreneurs 18 hrs / day

The overlapping term is whether you work at least 18 hrs. We'll have to contrapose our chain so that it's starting from "Work at least 18".

Work at → No time for → Not happy
least 18hrs Leisure entrepreneurs

So since we know that "Most *Successful Entrepreneurs* work at least 18 hrs a day", we know the other two ideas on this chain as well:

Most successful entrepreneurs have not time for leisure.
Most successful entrepreneurs are not happy.
  STEP 2 [Goal] beeb253f-0af4-4120-8a0c-f8bae841a937
The correct answer to Must Be False almost always either contradicts a conditional relationship we're given or contradicts an inference we can derive.

So we might see our inferences contradicted:
- Most successful do have leisure time
- Most successful are happy

Or we'll see our conditional chain contradicted:
- Someone works at least 18, but is happy.
(for example)

=== PT123-S3-Q14 | Principle-Conform | key C
STIM: Commentator: In academic scholarship, sources are always cited, and methodology and theoretical assumptions are set out, so as to allow critical study, replication, and expansion of scholarship. In open-source software, the code in which the program is written can be viewed and modified by individual users for their purposes without getting permission from the producer or paying a fee. In contrast, the code of proprietary software is kept secret, and modifications can be made only by the producer, for a fee. This shows that open-source software better matches the values embodied in academic scholarship, and since scholarship is central to the mission of universities, universities should use only open-source software.
STEM: The commentator's reasoning most closely conforms to which one of the following principles?
  A: Whatever software tools are most advanced and can achieve the goals of academic scholarship are the ones that should alone be used in universities.  [Out of Scope]
  B: Universities should use the type of software technology that is least expensive, as long as that type of software technology is adequate for the purposes of academic scholarship.  [Out of Scope]
  C: Universities should choose the type of software technology that best matches the values embodied in the activities that are central to the mission of universities.  [Correct]
  D: The form of software technology that best matches the values embodied in the activities that are central to the mission of universities is the form of software technology that is most efficient for universities to use.  [Out of Scope]
  E: A university should not pursue any activity that would block the achievement of the goals of academic scholarship at that university.  [Out of Scope]
BANK KEY C

=== PT123-S3-Q23 | Flaw | key D
STIM: Political candidates’ speeches are loaded with promises and with expressions of good intention, but one must not forget that the politicians’ purpose in giving these speeches is to get themselves elected. Clearly, then, these speeches are selfishly motivated and the promises made in them are unreliable.
STEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes a flaw in the argument above?
  A: The argument presumes, without providing justification, that if a person’s promise is not selfishly motivated then that promise is reliable.  [Negation]
  B: The argument presumes, without providing justification, that promises made for selfish reasons are never kept.  [Too Strong]
  C: The argument confuses the effect of an action with its cause.  [Wrong Flaw]
  D: The argument overlooks the fact that a promise need not be unreliable just because the person who made it had an ulterior motive for doing so.  [Correct]
  E: The argument overlooks the fact that a candidate who makes promises for selfish reasons may nonetheless be worthy of the office for which he or she is running.  [Wrong Conclusion]

=== PT126-S3-Q16 | Most Strongly Supported | key C
STIM: When a threat to life is common, as are automobile and industrial accidents, only unusual instances tend to be prominently reported by the news media. Instances of rare threats, such as product tampering, however, are seen as news by reporters and are universally reported in featured stories. People in general tend to estimate the risk of various threats by how frequently those threats come to their attention.
STEM: If the statements above are true, which one of the following is most strongly supported on the basis of them?
  A: Whether governmental action will be taken to lessen a common risk depends primarily on the prominence given to the risk by the news media.  [Out of Scope]
  B: People tend to magnify the risk of a threat if the threat seems particularly dreadful or if those who would be affected have no control over it.  [Out of Scope]
  C: Those who get their information primarily from the news media tend to overestimate the risk of uncommon threats relative to the risk of common threats.  [Correct]
  D: Reporters tend not to seek out information about long-range future threats but to concentrate their attention on the immediate past and future.  [Out of Scope]
  E: The resources that are spent on avoiding product tampering are greater than the resources that are spent on avoiding threats that stem from the weather.  [Out of Scope]
BANK KEY C
--- style single cc38882a-af24-4ead-b93e-54588e58eeca
  STEP 0 [Statements] bb2f61b3-86dc-4c7a-aa18-d7c5dfdc5461
Common threats like car and industrial accidents get reported only when unusual. Rare threats like product tampering get reported every time. People judge risk by how often a threat comes to their attention.
  STEP 1 [Evaluate] ed2e6b27-7fc1-4ab2-ace5-ddc613e5b6dd
Pause and infer by chaining the last sentence to the first two. Rare threats show up in the news every time; common threats mostly don't. People judge risk by how often they hear about a threat. So people who get their information from the news will see rare threats as bigger, compared with common ones, than they really are.
  STEP 2 [Goal] d0021f7a-fe50-42c2-87a3-8e427768247d
News-reliant people overestimate rare threats relative to common ones.
  NOTE A 977fe26d-948c-4a71-b997-5e8da74d1054
Governmental action to lessen a common risk is not discussed in the statements.
  NOTE B 5ca81bb0-1f06-47d9-baf5-2edf55ce6084
How dreadful a threat is and one’s ability to control a threat are not discussed in the statements.
  NOTE C 30f2fd6d-8bfe-44dd-b1f7-b7701f818fac
Rare threats are universally reported in the news media, while only unusual instances of common threats are reported in the media. Since the people this answer is talking about base their estimate of the risk of various threats by the frequency those threats appear in the media, people will overestimate rare threats relative to common ones.
  NOTE D 7ba90569-b5d5-4087-8e17-713a940cfdd3
The time horizon of a threat is not discussed in the statements.
  NOTE E cad0872c-b0d8-4427-9277-2a3c1f13e3d4
The resources spent to avoid specific threats are not discussed in the statements.

STIM: A strong correlation exists between what people value and the way they act. For example, those who value wealth tend to choose higher-paying jobs in undesirable locations over lower-paying jobs in desirable locations. Thus, knowing what people value can help one predict their actions.

  A: Knowing how people behave allows one to infer what they value.  [Reversal]
  B: People's claims concerning what they value are symptomatic of their actions.  [Out of Scope]
  C: No two people who value different things act the same way in identical circumstances.  [Too Strong]
  D: People who value wealth tend to allow their desire for it to outweigh other concerns.  [Evidence]
  E: What people value can be a reliable indicator of how they will act.  [Correct]

=== PT130-S4-Q3 | Main Conclusion | key A
STIM: The reason music with a simple recurring rhythm exerts a strong primordial appeal is that it reminds us of the womb environment. After all, the first sound heard within the womb is the comforting sound of the mother's regular heartbeat. So in taking away from us the warmth and security of the womb, birth also takes away a primal and constant source of comfort. Thus it is extremely natural that in seeking sensations of warmth and security throughout life, people would be strongly drawn toward simple recurring rhythmic sounds.

  E: The comforting sound of the mother's regular heartbeat is a simple recurring rhythmic sound.  [Assumption]

STIM: Columnist: A recent research report suggests that by exercising vigorously, one significantly lowers one's chances of developing certain cardio-respiratory illnesses. But exercise has this effect, the report concludes, only if the exercise is vigorous. Thus, one should not heed older studies purporting to show that nonstrenuous walking yields the same benefits.
STEM: The reasoning in the columnist's argument is most vulnerable to criticism on the grounds that this argument
  A: fails to consider the possibility that the risk of developing certain cardio-respiratory illnesses can be reduced by means other than exercise  [Out of Scope]
  B: fails to consider that those who exercise vigorously are at increased risk of physical injury caused by exercise  [Out of Scope]
  C: overlooks the possibility that vigorous exercise may prevent life-endangering diseases that have little to do with the cardio-respiratory system  [Out of Scope]
  D: fails to consider the possibility that those who engage in vigorous physical exercise are more likely than others to perceive themselves as healthy  [Out of Scope]
  E: fails to show that a certain conclusion of the recent report is better justified than an opposing conclusion reached in older studies  [Correct]
BANK KEY E

STIM: It is a mistake to think, as ecologists once did, that natural selection will eventually result in organisms that will be perfectly adapted to their environments. After all, perfect adaptation of an individual to its environment is impossible, for an individual's environment can vary tremendously; no single set of attributes could possibly prepare an organism to cope with all the conditions that it could face.

  A: When young children repeatedly hear a word without seeing the object denoted by the word, they sometimes apply the word to objects not denoted by the word.  [Wrong Trigger]

  C: The earlier in life a child encounters and uses an object, the easier it is for that child to learn how not to misuse the word denoting that object.  [Unsupported Comparison]

=== PT132-S4-Q1 | Main Conclusion | key C
STIM: Marine biologist: Scientists have long wondered why the fish that live around coral reefs exhibit such brilliant colors. One suggestion is that coral reefs are colorful and, therefore, that colorful fish are camouflaged by them. Many animal species, after all, use camouflage to avoid predators. However, as regards the populations around reefs, this suggestion is mistaken. A reef stripped of its fish is quite monochromatic. Most corals, it turns out, are relatively dull browns and greens.
STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses the main conclusion drawn in the marine biologist's argument?
  A: One hypothesis about why fish living near coral reefs exhibit such bright colors is that the fish are camouflaged by their bright colors.  [Opposing Point]
  B: The fact that many species use camouflage to avoid predators is one reason to believe that brightly colored fish living near reefs do too.  [Opposing Assumption]
  C: The suggestion that the fish living around coral reefs exhibit bright colors because they are camouflaged by the reefs is mistaken.  [Correct]
  D: A reef stripped of its fish is relatively monochromatic.  [Intermediate Conclusion]
  E: It turns out that the corals in a coral reef are mostly dull hues of brown and green.  [Premise]

=== PT133-S1-Q9 | Must Be True | key D
STIM: Computer scientist: For several decades, the number of transistors on new computer microchips, and hence the microchips' computing speed, has doubled about every 18 months. However, from the mid-1990s into the next decade, each such doubling in a microchip's computing speed was accompanied by a doubling in the cost of producing that microchip.
STEM: Which one of the following can be properly inferred from the computer scientist's statements?
  A: The only effective way to double the computing speed of computer microchips is to increase the number of transistors per microchip.  [Unsupported Relationship / Too Strong]
  B: From the mid-1990s into the next decade, there was little if any increase in the retail cost of computers as a result of the increased number of transistors on microchips.  [Out of Scope]
  C: For the last several decades, computer engineers have focused on increasing the computing speed of computer microchips without making any attempt to control the cost of producing them.  [Out of Scope]
  D: From the mid-1990s into the next decade, a doubling in the cost of fabricating new computer microchips accompanied each doubling in the number of transistors on those microchips.  [Correct]
  E: It is unlikely that engineers will ever be able to increase the computing speed of microchips without also increasing the cost of producing them.  [Speculation]
BANK KEY D

=== PT133-S1-Q13 | Main Conclusion | key D
STIM: A large company has been convicted of engaging in monopolistic practices. The penalty imposed on the company will probably have little if any effect on its behavior. Still, the trial was worthwhile, since it provided useful information about the company's practices. After all, this information has emboldened the company's direct competitors, alerted potential rivals, and forced the company to restrain its unfair behavior toward customers and competitors.

  A: Even if the company had not been convicted of engaging in monopolistic practices, the trial probably would have had some effect on the company's behavior.  [Speculation]

  C: The penalty imposed on the company will likely have little or no effect on its behavior.  [Opposing Point]
  D: The company's trial on charges of engaging in monopolistic practices was worthwhile.  [Correct]
  E: The penalty imposed on the company in the trial should have been larger.  [Unsupported]

STIM: Sometimes it is advisable for a medical patient to seek a second opinion. But this process can be awkward for both the patient and the physicians, since the patient often worries that the first physician will be alienated. In addition, for the first physician there is the issue of pride: a second opinion tacitly highlights a physician's fallibility. === PT134-S2-Q4 | Strengthen | key C
STIM: Editorialist: In a large corporation, one of the functions of the corporation's president is to promote the key interests of the shareholders. Therefore, the president has a duty to keep the corporation's profits high.

  A: Shareholders sometimes will be satisfied even if dividends paid to them from company profits are not high.  [Out of Scope]
  B: The president and the board of directors of a corporation are jointly responsible for advancing the key interests of the shareholders.  [Premise Support]
  C: Keeping a corporation's profits high is likely to advance the important interests of the corporation's shareholders.  [Correct]
  D: In considering where to invest, most potential shareholders are interested in more than just the profitability of a corporation.  [Out of Scope]
  E: The president of a corporation has many functions besides advancing the important interests of the corporation's shareholders.  [Premise Support]
  BANK C: Keeping a corporation’s profits high is likely to advance the important interests of the corporation’s shareholders.
  BANK E: The president of a corporation has many functions besides advancing the important interests of the corporation’s shareholders.
BANK KEY C

=== PT134-S2-Q11 | Paradox EXCEPT | key C
STIM: A recent study revealed that the percentage of people treated at large, urban hospitals who recover from their illnesses is lower than the percentage for people treated at smaller, rural hospitals.
STEM: Each of the following, if true, contributes to an explanation of the difference in recovery rates EXCEPT:
  A: Because there are fewer patients to feed, nutritionists at small hospitals are better able to tailor meals to the dietary needs of each patient.  [Possible Explanation]
  B: The less friendly, more impersonal atmosphere of large hospitals can be a source of stress for patients at those hospitals.  [Possible Explanation]
  C: Although large hospitals tend to draw doctors trained at the more prestigious schools, no correlation has been found between the prestige of a doctor's school and patients' recovery rate.  [Correct]
  D: Because space is relatively scarce in large hospitals, doctors are encouraged to minimize the length of time that patients are held for observation following a medical procedure.  [Possible Explanation]
  E: Doctors at large hospitals tend to have a greater number of patients and consequently less time to explain to staff and to patients how medications are to be administered.  [Possible Explanation]
  BANK C: Although large hospitals tend to draw doctors trained at the more prestigious schools, no correlation has been found between the prestige of a doctor’s school and patients’ recovery rate.
BANK KEY C

=== PT135-S1-Q2 | Main Conclusion | key A
STIM: Florist: Some people like to have green carnations on St. Patrick's Day. But flowers that are naturally green are extremely rare.   BANK C: There are some people who like to have green carnations on St. Patrick’s Day.
BANK KEY A

STIM: The best jazz singers use their voices much as horn players use their instruments. The great Billie Holiday thought of her singing voice as a horn, reshaping melody and words to increase their impact. Conversely, jazz horn players achieve their distinctive sounds by emulating the spontaneous twists and turns of an impassioned voice. So jazz consists largely of voicelike horns and hornlike voices.
STEM: Which one of the following most accurately describes the role played in the argument by the claim that the best jazz singers use their voices much as horn players use their instruments?
  A: It is the argument's main conclusion and is supported by another statement, which is itself supported by a further statement.  [Wrong Role]
  B: It is the argument's only conclusion, and each of the other statements in the argument is used to support it.  [Contradiction]
  C: It is a statement for which some evidence is provided and which in turn is used to provide support for the argument's main conclusion.  [Correct]
  D: It is a statement for which no evidence is provided but which itself is used to support the argument's only conclusion.  [Contradiction]
  E: It is a statement used to support a conclusion that in turn is used to support the argument's main conclusion.  [Contradiction]
  BANK A: It is the argument’s main conclusion and is supported by another statement, which is itself supported by a further statement.
  BANK B: It is the argument’s only conclusion, and each of the other statements in the argument is used to support it.
  BANK C: It is a statement for which some evidence is provided and which in turn is used to provide support for the argument’s main conclusion.
  BANK D: It is a statement for which no evidence is provided but which itself is used to support the argument’s only conclusion.
  BANK E: It is a statement used to support a conclusion that in turn is used to support the argument’s main conclusion.
BANK KEY C

=== PT135-S4-Q14 | Main Conclusion | key E
STIM: Geographer: Because tropical storms require heat and moisture, they form especially over ocean surfaces of at least 26 degrees Celsius (79 degrees Fahrenheit), ocean temperatures that global warming would encourage. For this reason, many early discussions of global warming predicted that it would cause more frequent and intense tropical storms. But recent research shows that this prediction is unlikely to be borne out. Other factors, such as instabilities in wind flow, are likely to counteract global warming's effects on tropical storm development.

=== PT142-S2-Q13 | Sufficient Assumption | key B
STIM: Critic: Photographers, by deciding which subjects to depict and how to depict them, express their own worldviews in their photographs, however realistically those photographs may represent reality. Thus, photographs are interpretations of reality.
STEM: The argument's conclusion is properly drawn if which one of the following is assumed?
  A: Even representing a subject realistically can involve interpreting that subject.  [Too Weak]
  B: To express a worldview is to interpret reality.  [Correct]
  C: All visual art expresses the artist's worldview.  [Supports a Premise]
  D: Any interpretation of reality involves the expression of a worldview.  [Reversal]
  E: Nonrealistic photographs, like realistic photographs, express the world views of the photographers who take them.  [Out of Scope / Supports a Premise]
  BANK C: All visual art expresses the artist’s worldview.
  BANK E: Nonrealistic photographs, like realistic photographs, express the worldviews of the photographers who take them.
BANK KEY B

=== PT143-S4-Q16 | Main Conclusion | key C

=== PT144-S3-Q18 | Main Conclusion | key A
STIM: Field studies, which have long been a staple of anthropological research, involve the researcher living within the community being studied. However, the usefulness of field studies tends to be overrated by anthropologists. Although most anthropologists do realize that living within the community one is studying affects that community, they generally underestimate the extent of such effects.
STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses the conclusion drawn in the argument?
  A: Anthropologists tend to overestimate the value of field studies.  [Correct]
  B: In a field study, the researcher lives within the community being studied.  [Evidence]
  C: Field studies have been a central feature of anthropological research for a long time.  [Evidence]
  D: Most anthropologists know that when they live within a community being studied, the community is affected at least somewhat.  [Opposing Point]
  E: Most anthropologists underestimate how much of an effect the researcher's presence has on a community being studied.  [Evidence]
  BANK E: Most anthropologists underestimate how much of an effect the researcher’s presence has on a community being studied.
BANK KEY A

=== PT147-S4-Q4 | Sufficient Assumption | key A
STIM: Economist: Unemployment will soon decrease. If total government spending significantly increases next year, the economy will be stimulated in the short term and unemployment will decrease. If, on the other hand, total government spending significantly decreases next year, businesses will retain more of their earnings in the short term and employ more workers, thereby decreasing unemployment.
STEM: The conclusion drawn by the economist is properly inferred if which one of the following is assumed?
  A: Either total government spending will significantly decrease next year or else total government spending will significantly increase next year.  [Correct]
  B: Government officials are currently implementing policies that are intended to reduce unemployment.  [Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending]
  C: If there is a significantly increased demand for workers, then there will be a significant decrease in unemployment.  [Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending]
  D: A significant increase in total government spending will slow the economy in the long run.  [Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending]
  E: If the economy is not stimulated and businesses do not retain more of their earnings, then unemployment will not decrease.  [Unrelated to Goal: nothing about spending]
BANK KEY A
--- style single 40ae62ac-de80-47c8-baec-cfabe66715ef
  STEP 0 [Conclusion] b8630686-1d8d-4b91-bb33-e7c148dc0ee4
Unemployment will soon decrease.
  STEP 1 [Evidence] 1cef0c96-8d24-400f-8898-c1da4fa516e0
If total govt spending significantly goes up, unemployment will decrease.

If total govt spending significantly decreases, unemployment will decrease.
  STEP 2 [Evaluate] e8c85123-28f1-4b94-801f-fcc6fe3a8a11
We ascertain that the first claim is the conclusion by seeing that the other two claims are clearly meant to work together. Conjunctions/connections like "and / but / on the other hand" show us that two claims are interacting together to move the argument forward.

Each of the conditional premises involves TWO consequences, one of which is decreasing unemployment. I didn't bother writing out the other half of each outcome, because all we care about is proving that there will be decreasing unemployment.

At first, this argument seems pretty reasonable. It seems like an *Either Way You Slice It* style of argument: "Whether spending increases significantly or decreases significantly, we end up with decreasing unemployment. So either way you slice it, we're gonna have decreasing unemployment."

But we know that an argument on Sufficient Assumption can't *already* be airtight. There has to be some gap, so that the correct answer can make it airtight.

How would unemployment *fail to decrease*?

Well, I guess spending doesn't necessarily have to go significantly up or significantly down. It could more or less stay the same.

The author is presenting a *False Choice* (a false binary / a false dichotomy), acting like spending will either go up a bunch or down a bunch.

But she's failing to consider the middle ground, where spending basically stays the same.
  STEP 3 [Goal] 0cf755b5-c56e-473b-8db9-5095d3dd3436
We just need to rule out the possibility that govt spending will stay basically the same. We need to know that it'll definitely go up or down significantly.
  NOTE A b7a65957-2e87-44e7-a774-b4de9da7ce21
The economist covers two cases: spending goes up significantly, or spending goes down significantly. Both lead to lower unemployment. But nothing says one of those two things will happen. Spending could stay about the same, and then neither rule applies.

This answer closes that gap. If spending will either rise or fall significantly, one of the two cases must happen, and either way unemployment decreases. The conclusion follows.
  NOTE B e643f423-995d-406d-8018-f6de787bf37d
Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
  NOTE C 38715b68-3d82-447a-9fb4-f83157a24af1
Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will gone up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
  NOTE D 3edb70f9-f304-41d1-a775-ab6b64837d6c
Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.
  NOTE E 74abf194-80aa-4ac4-b219-d502bca5f8fc
Since this doesn't tell us whether spending will go up or down, it doesn't do the job we need it to do.

=== PT148-S3-Q19 | Agree/Disagree | key C
STIM: Ana: On libertarian principles, I oppose the proposed smoking ban. It is not the government's business to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves.

Pankaj: But keep in mind that the ban would apply only to smoking in public places. People could still smoke all they want in private.
STEM: The dialogue provides the most support for the claim that Ana and Pankaj disagree over whether
  A: it is the government's business to prevent people from harming themselves  [Out of Scope Person 2]
  B: government should be restrained by libertarian principles  [Out of Scope Person 2]
  C: the proposed smoking ban is intended to prevent harm only to smokers themselves  [Correct]
  D: the proposed ban would prohibit smoking in public places  [Unknown Person 1]
  E: there are cases in which government should attempt to regulate private behavior  [Out of Scope Person 2]
  BANK A: it is the government’s business to prevent people from harming themselves
BANK KEY C
--- style single bec64040-7b47-40b1-b728-b9e4400c8cc1
  STEP 0 [Ana's 1st Claim] 1065ffe0-ba4e-4097-bc0b-700539237a92
On libertarian, principles, I oppose the proposed smoking ban.
*Does Pankaj say he does not oppose the smoking ban?*

No, nothing definitive. He pushes back against Ana's opposition, but that doesn't necessarily mean that he supports the ban. On LSAT, it's common for someone to disagree with person A's rationale for reaching position X, even though they don't necessarily disagree with position X.
  STEP 1 [Ana's 2nd Claim] ca6d1705-3f5c-4664-8d1c-488a1a167d09
It isn't the government's business to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves.
*Does Pankaj say he thinks that it* **is** *the government's business sometimes to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves?*

No, he doesn't say anything about the government or what its role is.
  STEP 2 [Evaluate] 86bd2159-9263-434f-8549-6e0e40e2259d
Since Pankaj didn't disagree with any of Ana's explicit claims, we should start asking ourselves what Ana was *assuming*, because maybe Pankaj is disagreeing with an assumption.

The connection between Ana's two sentences assumes that "the proposed smoking ban *is trying to prevent people from doing things that harm only themselves*".

Does Pankaj disagree with this?

Yes! He's saying that people are still allowed to smoke all they want in private (it's not preventing people from doing things that harm only themselves). The ban applies only to smoking public spaces (so it's aimed at preventing people from doing things that harm *others*, in this case preventing cigarette smokers from harming others with their secondhand smoke).
  STEP 3 [Goal] 4f316b8c-be35-4d13-8f21-b59ae16185c2
Look for something like "Whether the ban is preventing people from doing things that harm only themselves".
  NOTE A e6e3674d-1b5b-4e8a-bab7-82c680153aa8
As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.
  NOTE B 0ac01bec-dc9d-45df-bbe7-cc2615ebf4af
As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.
  NOTE C c40a32c3-0ce4-4b22-a830-6fc26f568985
Ana would agree with this. It's implied by her statements that she opposes the ban because she thinks it's intended to prevent harm only to smokers themselves and she rejects that on libertarian principles.

Panjak would disagree with this, because by pointing out that you are banned from smoking in public places but not in private, he's implying that the ban is targeting the effects of smoking on others not on the smoker him/herself.
  NOTE D c9c7d3fd-6a1d-4bbb-a8c5-5d180dd7bb40
Pankaj would clearly agree with this because he explicitly says this. Can we derive from Ana's paragraph that she thinks that "the proposed ban would not prohibit smoking in public places"?

No, we can't derive that. She may think that the smoking ban applies to public places (as well as private places).

If we changed this answer to say "the proposed ban would prohibit smoking only in public places", then we could probably pick it.
  NOTE E 05b3361f-7bf0-4d38-be89-b3ff19bec065
As we analyzed, Pankaj makes no statements about the government at all.

=== PT148-S4-Q2 | Agree/Disagree | key C
STIM: Bell: Commentators in the media are wrong to criticize the policies Klein implemented. Although her policies are unpopular, they avoided an impending catastrophe. Klein is just the person we need making important decisions in the future.

Soltan: Klein's policies have been effective, but politics matters. In the future, important decisions will need to be made, and she will not have the political support to make them. So she should step down.
STEM: Bell and Soltan disagree with each other about which one of the following?
  A: Klein's policies have been effective.  [Both Agree]
  B: Klein's policies are unpopular.  [Nobody Disagrees]
  C: Klein should step down.  [Correct]
  D: There are important decisions to be made in the future.  [Both Agree]
  E: Klein's policies were implemented in the face of an impending catastrophe.  [Nobody Disagrees]
  BANK A: Klein’s policies have been effective.
  BANK B: Klein’s policies are unpopular.
  BANK E: Klein’s policies were implemented in the face of an impending catastrophe.
BANK KEY C
--- style single dfdb259f-4a76-4cd7-aa1a-45bdf5097d30
  STEP 0 [Given Bell's Claims] e13a3185-4cc6-45f0-b65a-e636faf3f1a2
1. Commentators in the media are wrong to criticize the policies Klein implemented.

2. Klein's policies are unpopular.

3. Klein's policies avoided an impending catastrophe.

4. Klein is just the person we need making important decisions in the future.
  STEP 1 [Evaluate Soltan's Responses] e43489ef-03d0-4a44-80c9-b80959f7ed81
1. no comment
2. potentially agrees
3. potentially agrees
4. disagrees

For claim #2, by saying, "Klein's policies have been effective," Soltan is potentially agreeing with Bell that Klein's policies avoided an impending catastrophe. And by saying "she will not have the political support to make them," Soltan potentially agrees that Klein's policies are unpopular. Neither of these are definite, but at best they would be points that both speakers **agree** on. We're being asked for their point of disagreement, and there's no evidence that they disagree about claim #2 or claim #3.
  STEP 2 [Goal] b498406b-c557-42f7-8ae9-4e69832345d3
We'll look for an answer that addresses claim #4. Beware of answers that describe claim #2 or #3, which are potential points of agreement, not disagreement.
  NOTE A 4fa2c23e-fdc1-4441-b30b-219fca4d4d11
Soltan states this. Bell states that Klein's policies avoided an impending catastrophe.
  NOTE B 897ad25b-bfaa-461f-b618-99b556e27fdc
Bell states this. Soltan never disagrees. In fact, by stating that "politics matters" and that Klein will not have the political support to make important decisions, Soltan appears to be agreeing with Bell.
  NOTE C b9cbdb41-4d2c-47ee-a0db-3a1c53b6085c
Soltan states this. Bell disagrees, saying Klein "is just the person we need making important decisions in the future."
  NOTE D bf8203de-b323-48c3-a910-9e65ae4d022b
Soltan states this, and Bell talks about Klein making important decisions in the future.
  NOTE E 26c9d0bd-7d8f-4d1a-a6e8-1f84a9423f94
Bell states that Klein's policies "avoided an impending catastrophe." Soltan never disputes this.

=== PT108-S3-Q6 | Paradox EXCEPT | key C
STIM: Shortly after the power plant opened and began discharging its wastewater into the river, there was a decrease in the overall number of bass caught by anglers downstream from the plant.
STEM: Each of the following, if true, could explain the decrease described above EXCEPT:
  A: The discharged wastewater made the river more attractive to fish that are the natural predators of bass.  [Helps Explain]
  B: The discharged water was warmer than the normal river temperature, leading the bass to seek cooler water elsewhere.  [Helps Explain]
  C: Because of the new plant, access to the part of the river downstream from the plant was improved, leading to an increase in the number of anglers fishing for bass.  [Correct]
  D: Because of the new plant, the level of noise downstream increased, making that section of the river a less popular place for anglers to fish.  [Helps Explain]
  E: The discharged wastewater created turbulence that disrupted the vegetation of the river downstream, destroying some of the bass's natural habitat.  [Helps Explain]
BANK KEY C
--- style single 7aca97bc-3629-4277-bbd4-5597bdda2c4c
  STEP 0 [Given that ...] 020f306b-a3e9-4046-8b8f-8e2726f19554
The power plant opened and started discharging wastewater into the river.
  STEP 1 [Why is it that ...] 78fa0921-1787-4fe5-870a-f74547986e47
Shortly after, anglers downstream caught fewer bass overall?
  STEP 2 [Evaluate] 30050c0b-3c74-407f-a7ab-42bd91a5178d
This isn't a true paradox. Nothing here is surprising. The stem just wants explanations for a drop in the number of bass caught.

Think about what "number of bass caught" depends on. It takes two things: bass in the water and people fishing for them. So there are two roads to fewer bass caught:
1. **Fewer bass downstream.** They died, got eaten, moved away, or lost their habitat.
2. **Less fishing downstream.** Fewer anglers, or anglers fishing less.

Usually, on EXCEPT questions, three of the four answers we eliminate come at the stimulus from one angle, and the fourth takes a different tactic. Here, expect three answers on road 1 and one sneaking in on road 2.

Most students only think of road 1, because the wastewater feels like it must be hurting the fish. But road 2 is just as good. If half the anglers stop showing up, half as many bass get caught, even if the bass are thriving.
  STEP 3 [Goal] 3a915989-8077-4179-8156-11839c6a5952
Eliminate four answers that explain fewer bass caught, by either road. The correct answer will do neither, or it will push the other way: more bass or more fishing, which should mean *more* bass caught.

Be careful with any answer about anglers. It isn't out of scope. Anglers are half the equation.
  NOTE A bc1e9cd3-871c-463a-ae9e-14108e66a53e
There are fewer bass to be caught, because an increase in predators has depleted the bass population.
  NOTE B 09715b96-d8dc-43ac-9da7-8df2738d6c9d
There are fewer bass to be caught, because the bass have swum upstream or far downstream to cooler water.
  NOTE C a7363b92-0e04-4d9f-92f5-559a26c2300a
If there are more people fishing, we'd expect MORE bass to be caught. This doesn't explain why FEWER bass are being caught.
  NOTE D 96597a73-05b1-4189-b7a8-85bb2b514718
There are fewer bass being caught, because there are fewer anglers fishing downstream of the plant.
  NOTE E 6b186f39-db7c-4cdb-811b-9d07def23aa5
There are fewer bass to be caught, because destruction of the habitat downstream from the plant has led to a smaller population of bass in the area.

=== PT122-S1-Q9 | Most Strongly Supported | key B
STIM: Technological improvements will enable food production to increase as populations increase. However, increases in food production will be negligible unless societies become more centralized so that all factors contributing to the production of food can be better coordinated. But, historically, the more centralized a society was, the greater the percentage of its people who perished if and when it collapsed. Thus, increasing the centralization of societies in an effort to increase food production via better technology will merely exacerbate the disasters associated with societal collapse.
STEM: The statements above, if true, most strongly support which one of the following?
  A: The more centralized a society is, the greater its need for increased food production.  [Too Strong: volume dial / Unsupported Relationship]
  B: Not every problem associated with the collapse of a centralized society would be prevented by technological improvements.  [Correct]
  C: The rate at which the world’s population is growing will continue to increase indefinitely.  [Too Strong: will grow indefinitely]
  D: The production of food can be increased only by improved technology.  [Too Strong: only by]
  E: Societies have become more centralized as technology has improved.  [Too Strong: volume dial / Unsupported Relationship]
  BANK C: The rate at which the world's population is growing will continue to increase indefinitely.
BANK KEY B

  STEP 0 [Statements] f0cd6b85-6d3e-4cf1-9372-6d9978ea7d86
Technology could raise food production, but only negligibly unless societies centralize. Historically, the more centralized a society, the more of its people died when it collapsed. So centralizing to boost food production will make collapses worse.
  STEP 1 [Evaluate] 718bd96e-cea7-49a0-b1b6-9a55ffe0fb3b
Pause and infer. Technology brings more food, but the centralization it needs makes collapse deadlier. So technology doesn't remove the dangers of collapse. If anything, the route to more food makes them worse.
  STEP 2 [Goal] 7c2f4e9a-fca0-4474-a21b-87372a6535a2
Technology won't prevent every problem that comes with a centralized society's collapse.
  NOTE A b465e72d-0902-4d8a-8703-0ed208525611
This is the most popular wrong answer. The passage ties centralization to deaths *in a collapse*, not to a greater need for food. Food production is what drives centralization, not the other way around.
  NOTE B 9ad6805f-2308-4a22-a1e5-2f6a4117aead
Centralizing for more food makes collapses worse, so technology plainly doesn't fix every collapse problem. "Not every" asks for very little.
  NOTE C 95d2ac70-01e5-4301-bab1-276df99e9c34
Population growth continuing "indefinitely" is far beyond anything here.
  NOTE D dce8601f-8057-4639-996a-c9a8db1a766b
"Only by technology" is never claimed.
  NOTE E d2087976-f065-4a03-9d00-53af8db03539
The passage gives no history of centralization rising with technology. It talks about what *would* happen.

=== PT147-S4-Q6 | Necessary Assumption | key B
STIM: Scientist: Laboratory animals have access to ample food, and they get relatively little exercise. These factors can skew the results of research using animals, since such studies often rely on the assumption that the animal subjects are healthy. For instance, animal studies that purport to show that extreme caloric restriction can extend lifespans take for granted that their subjects were not overfed to begin with.
STEM: The scientist's argument requires assuming which one of the following?
  A: Laboratory animals are healthy if they are fed a carefully restricted diet and get plenty of exercise.  [Illegal Negation]
  B: Laboratory conditions that provide animals with ample food but relatively little exercise can be unhealthy for the animals.  [Correct]
  C: It is not unusual for animals outside of laboratory settings to have access to ample food and get relatively little exercise.  [Out of Scope: outside of lab settings]
  D: Some animal studies take into consideration the differences between the living conditions of laboratory animals and those of other animals.  [Out of Scope: outside of lab settings / Opposite (if anything): taking into consideration]
  E: When provided with unlimited food over a long period of time, animals show little day-to-day variation in their eating habits.  [Out of Scope: unlimited food]
BANK KEY B
--- style single 45d92455-5b00-4472-bb31-9a00e8dc6c24
  STEP 0 [Conclusion] 59f38650-9061-45b7-bf88-dded829d06dd
Ample food and little exercise can skew the results of animal research.
  STEP 1 [Evidence] 6b347fd3-189c-46ec-b328-17d5d44896df
Lab animals get ample food and little exercise. Many studies assume their subjects are healthy. The caloric restriction studies, for example, assume their animals were not overfed to begin with.
  STEP 2 [Evaluate] 4f3e4aca-04cf-45ce-9d7f-d0d0d71eeca5
The skewing only happens if lab conditions make the animals *unhealthy*. If eating freely and exercising little left them perfectly healthy, the studies' assumption would be true, and nothing would be skewed. The scientist never says the conditions are bad for the animals.
  STEP 3 [Goal] f660ba3d-c14b-4e22-a4a5-e485d80a4b49
A link: ample food and little exercise can be unhealthy for lab animals.
  NOTE A 0c6d5556-a811-4bc3-baf7-ce13b161f6d5
This is the most popular wrong answer. It flips the scientist's idea: the scientist needs bad conditions to *cause* poor health, not good conditions to *guarantee* good health. A restricted diet and plenty of exercise could still leave an animal unhealthy for other reasons, and the argument would be fine.
  NOTE B 9afe9cb1-0652-4a44-948f-90b0bac54314
Negate it: "these conditions *cannot* be unhealthy for the animals." Then the animals are healthy, the studies' assumption holds, and there is nothing to skew the results.
  NOTE C 9659c2e3-53c6-426d-b6e9-c52b170844b2
Animals outside the lab are not part of the argument.
  NOTE D 141cd8cd-9fab-4f13-bc3a-c50ea68c1e6d
What some studies take into account does not affect whether the conditions can skew results.
  NOTE E 4cfc1100-804b-40eb-a325-b3203c009063
How steady their eating habits are says nothing about whether the habits are healthy.

=== PT147-S4-Q3 | Paradox | key D
STIM: Hospital patients generally have lower infection rates and require shorter hospital stays if they are housed in private rooms rather than semiprivate rooms. Yet in Woodville's hospital, which has only semiprivate rooms, infection rates and length of stays are typically the same as in several nearby hospitals where most of the rooms are private, even though patients served by these hospitals are very similar to those served by Woodville's hospital.
STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to resolve the apparent conflict in the information above?
  A: Many of the doctors who routinely treat patients in Woodville's hospital also routinely treat patients in one or more of the nearby hospitals.  [No Distinction / Opposite (if anything)]
  B: Most of the nearby hospitals were built within the last 10 years, whereas Woodville's hospital was built about 50 years ago.  [Opposite (if anything)]
  C: Infection is more likely to be spread where people come into close contact with one another than where they do not.  [Restates Paradox / Unrelated to Goal]
  D: Woodville's hospital has a policy of housing one patient per room in semiprivate rooms whenever possible.  [Correct]
  E: Woodville's hospital is located in its central business district, whereas most of the nearby hospitals are located outside their municipalities' business districts.  [Unclear Impact / Thin Ice]
  BANK A: Many of the doctors who routinely treat patients in Woodville’s hospital also routinely treat patients in one or more of the nearby hospitals.
  BANK B: Most of the nearby hospitals were built within the last 10 years, whereas Woodville’s hospital was built about 50 years ago.
  BANK D: Woodville’s hospital has a policy of housing one patient per room in semiprivate rooms whenever possible.
  BANK E: Woodville’s hospital is located in its central business district, whereas most of the nearby hospitals are located outside their municipalities’ business districts.
BANK KEY D
--- style single 46c441f2-55b0-43bd-8d9f-ff39165a1ad6
  STEP 0 [Given that] d0121991-85a5-4f08-ba09-1400c6260735
Patients in private rooms generally get fewer infections and shorter stays than patients in semiprivate rooms.
  STEP 1 [How can it be that] 97867ed9-4676-4509-a029-97c28772e0c9
Woodville's hospital has only semiprivate rooms, yet its infection rates and stays match nearby hospitals with mostly private rooms and very similar patients?
  STEP 2 [Evaluate] 6ce9a2c6-21f1-4b5e-ae7b-5b4e9e47724f
The patients are similar, so look at how the rooms are used. A semiprivate room is built for two, but if Woodville often puts only one patient in it, its patients get the benefit of a private room.
  STEP 3 [Goal] a54f3f11-a758-43fc-b61b-6ea18276567d
Find a difference between Woodville and the nearby hospitals that gives Woodville's patients the benefits of private rooms.
  NOTE A 8431f89a-1c8e-4cef-83bf-891a618b49f4
We want a distinction between Woodville and nearby hospitals, and this provides a similarity.
  NOTE B 8d3a9954-93a4-4860-8ac7-f58b506d60b1
We want a reason why Woodville is doing better than we'd expect, and being told that it is an older hospital seems more to be a reason why it would do worse than one might expect.
  NOTE C 811c847b-7416-4a0d-ad9d-2b8e8a5bb907
This is reinforcing the background claim, explaining why private rooms tend to lead to lower infection rates and shorter hospital stays.
It does nothing to explain the surprise, which is why Woodville's semiprivate rooms seem to be doing as well as other hospitals' private rooms.
  NOTE D c6089d54-79b2-469d-b09e-cc79015781f3
We want a reason Woodville's semiprivate rooms do as well as other hospitals' private rooms, and this gives it.

If Woodville puts one patient in each semiprivate room whenever it can, then in practice most of its patients get a room to themselves. So Woodville gets the benefits of private rooms without having any, and the surprise goes away.
  NOTE E b31dce01-5a21-4d11-95a9-6bacb7aa43f9
For this to explain the surprise, we'd have to think that being located in the central business district has some common sense connection to lower infection rates or shorter hospital stays, which would definitely be walking on thin ice.

=== PT133-S2-Q25 | Strengthen | key C
STIM: In ancient Greece, court witnesses were not cross-examined and the jury, selected from the citizenry, received no guidance on points of law; thus, it was extremely important for litigants to make a good impression on the jurors. For this reason, courtroom oratory by litigants is a good source of data on the common conceptions of morality held by the citizens of ancient Greece.
STEM: Which one of the following, if true, would most strengthen the argument?
  A: Litigants believed jurors were more likely to be impressed by litigants whose personality they preferred.  [Out of Scope - Term Shift]
  B: Litigants believed jurors were more likely to subject the litigants' personal moral codes to close critical scrutiny than were people who did not sit on juries.  [Too Weak]
  C: Litigants believed jurors were likely to be impressed by litigants whose professed moral code most resembled their own.  [Correct]
  D: Litigants believed jurors to be more impressed by litigants who were of the same economic class as the jurors.  [Weakens]
  E: Litigants believed jurors were likely to render their decisions based on a good understanding of the law.  [Weakens]
BANK KEY C

=== PT108-S3-Q16 | Principle-Conform | key C
STIM: To act responsibly in one's professional capacity, one must act on the basis of information that one has made a reasonable effort to make sure is accurate and complete.
STEM: Which one of the following judgments most closely conforms to the principle cited above?
  A: Peggy acted responsibly in ordering new computers for the school last year because they turned out to be needed due to an unexpected increase in enrollment this year.  [Bad Conclusion Match]
  B: Mary acted responsibly in firing John, for she first examined the details of his work record and listened to negative reports from some of his supervisors and coworkers.  [Bad Conclusion Match]
  C: Toril did not act responsibly in investing the company's money in Twicycled Ink, for, though the investment yielded a large return, she had not investigated the risks associated with that investment.  [None]
  D: Conchita did not act responsibly in hiring Helmer to do the company's bookkeeping because Helmer made a mistake that cost the company a lot of money, though he had never been known to make such a mistake in the past.  [Bad Trigger Match]
  E: Jennifer did not act responsibly in deciding where to go on her vacation because, instead of carefully weighing her options, she waited until the last minute and decided on impulse.  [Bad Trigger Match]
BANK STIM DIFF: To act responsibly in one’s professional capacity, one must act on the basis of information that one has made a reasonable effort to make sure is accurate and complete.
  BANK C: Toril did not act responsibly in investing the company’s money in Twicycled Ink, for, though the investment yielded a large return, she had not investigated the risks associated with that investment.
  BANK D: Conchita did not act responsibly in hiring Helmer to do the company’s bookkeeping because Helmer made a mistake that cost the company a lot of money, though he had never been known to make such a mistake in the past.
BANK KEY C
--- style single b0c57730-1537-4a9c-b918-8e947c7fc1f2
  STEP 0 [Principle] 11b5d5bc-e67a-4d8d-8d11-7a3e4a401a96
To do X, one must do Y is equivalent to saying X requires Y, which is equivalent to writing X → Y.
So we could write this principle like this
Acting responsibly Acting on the basis of in your job's role → info you're reasonably sure is true / complete
But if we're savvy about Apply the Principle questions, we know we want the "judgy" term on the right. Principles are supposed to sound like, "if this description applies to you, then this value judgment applies to you".
Since "You acted responsibly / you acted irresponsibly" is the value judgment half of this answer, we want to contrapose the rule so that it's on the right side, and will thus match the conclusion of the arguments in the answer choices.
If you weren't acting then you weren't on info you tried to → acting responsibly make sure was true in your professional and accurate capacity
  STEP 1 [Goal] 85526f14-fbaa-490e-b62b-f412fd6b9726

If you weren't acting then you weren't on info you tried to → acting responsibly make sure was true in your professional and accurate capacity

If you weren't acting then you weren't on info you tried to → acting responsibly make sure was true in your professional and accurate capacity
  NOTE C 93db19fe-b077-459d-a02e-c30f283f8c2e
The conclusion says Toril did not act responsibly, so this choice is worth reading. Does the evidence say Toril didn't make a reasonable effort to get accurate and complete information? Yes: Toril invested the company's money without investigating the risks. Investing company money is a professional act, so the principle applies. The large return doesn't matter. The rule judges the effort made before acting, not how things turned out.
  NOTE D 8eaa07b1-10bc-4621-93e8-74b75537827c
This is concluding the person did not act responsibly, so it's worth reading. Does the evidence establish that Conchita didn't act on well-verified information?

If you weren't acting then you weren't on info you tried to → acting responsibly make sure was true in your professional and accurate capacity

Not really. It doesn't say one way or the other whether Conchita made a reasonable effort to make sure she was acting on true / complete info, but in order for an answer to be correct it needs to tell us explicitly that she wasn't acting on info she verified was good.

The fact that Helmer made a mistake he had never made before is consistent with the idea that she did do her due diligence and Helmer had nothing like this in his past.
  NOTE E 7ebd7168-911f-4d47-b790-07ef84ab2fed
This is concluding the person did not act responsibly, so it's worth reading. Does the evidence establish that Jennifer didn't act on well-verified information?

Yes, it does. She acted on impulse, not well-verified info.

And was she acting irresponsibly in her professional capacity? No, she was just booking vacation, which sounds like a personal decision, so this principle doesn't apply.

=== PT146-S3-Q11 | Must Be False | key E
STIM: From 1880 to 2000 Britain’s economy grew fivefold, but emissions of carbon dioxide, a greenhouse gas, were the same on a per capita basis in Britain in 2000 as they were in 1880.
STEM: The claims made above are incompatible with which one of the following generalizations?
  A: A decrease in per capita emissions of carbon dioxide never occurs during a period of economic growth.  [Compatible]
  B: Countries whose economies are growing slowly or not at all usually cannot afford to enact laws restricting carbon dioxide emissions.  [Compatible / Out of Scope: afford laws]
  C: Economic growth initially leads to increased per capita emissions of greenhouse gases, but eventually new technologies are developed that tend to reduce these emissions.  [Compatible]
  D: As the world’s population grows, emissions of greenhouse gases will increase proportionately.  [Out of Scope: world population]
  E: Economic growth always increases household income and consumption, which inevitably increases per capita emissions of carbon dioxide.  [Correct]
BANK KEY E
--- style single b86facc4-a85e-4acd-b2a7-fae411fe5c7a
  STEP 0 [Statements (Math-y Pivot)] 19ae1e5b-c684-4122-8d44-d7a3e53635d7
1880 vs. 2000
  Britain's economy 5x bigger
  but
  Per capita emissions same
  STEP 1 [Goal] 8c6e83a2-26b4-4994-8c6a-62e5dcf72a85
Since they're giving us a generalization (similar to a Principle), it might be conditional in nature. If you're violating a conditional, then you provide an example in which the left side is true, but the right side isn't true.
  If, for example, we had a principle like
  "if your economy is 5 times bigger than before, then your per capita emissions will be higher than before",
  this example would be violating it.
  Britain's economy was 5 times bigger,
  but per capita emissions weren't higher.
  NOTE A 0d06ed79-e534-41b4-96f3-02d96d8726b3
This rule would diagram like this:
```
period of → no decrease in emissions
econ growth
```
An incompatible situation would be one in which it was a period of economic growth, and there was a decrease in per capita emission (PCE).

But out situation didn't involve a decrease in PCE.
  NOTE B d5774404-bccd-4228-baa4-ca8a60abbd11
An incompatible situation for this principle,
Growing slowly → can't afford emissions laws

would involve a situation where
- the economy was growing slowly
and
- they could afford to enact emissions laws.

The stuff we heard about Britain didn't establish either of those ideas.
  NOTE C db7ceeb4-2fdb-442c-b0ea-ebed4f5ad8e6
Since Britain's economic growth quintupled, this generalization would expect that emissions would initially go up, but then new technology would save the day and reduce the per capita emissions level.

Britain's 1880 emissions level is the same as its 2000 level, but we don't need the emissions level to have been constantly the same throughout those 120 years.

This answer choice tells an econ growth / emissions level story that could very well have applied to Britain. It certainly doesn't contradict what happened in Britain. It allows for a period of economic growth eventually ending up at the emissions level where it started.
  NOTE D 44882910-b6bf-4a4c-a727-a4d1b540f0bc
The facts are about per capita emissions in Britain. Total emissions could still have grown with the population, which fits this generalization. Nothing contradicts it.
  NOTE E 605c0d81-3e71-4b94-80b4-095bd24966e2
This is a three part principle, but we will still violate it by showing a situation in which the left side is true, but the right side isn't.

econ growth → more house $ → higher emissions

In Britain, there was economic growth (5x the 1880 economy) but comparable levels of per capita emissions.

##### 548e7ef2-bbe6-492a-a041-db16578b8a2a
This paragraph doesn't read too much as an *argument*, but the first sentence *is* an opinion ("superior"). Our usual goal when we're matching a Principle to an argument is to make the conclusion be the outcome of the principle, and a consideration from the evidence to be the trigger of the principle.
  If the conclusion is saying "one system is superior to another", then we want a principle that helps us judge the *superiority* of a theory.
  Why does our author think C's theory was better than P's theory?
  P posited something that C found unlikely. And C ** thought of a simpler theory.
  We obviously know that C's theory is borne out as the correct one, but why did the author find it superior *at the time it was proposed*, even though the available evidence was equally consistent with both theories?
  The superiority must have been its simplicity. That's the famous principle known as Ockham's Razor (the literal phrasing is something like "avoid superfluous entities", but the usage is that "all other things being equal, the simpler explanation is the better one").

##### 3f9093c9-4287-49d6-b227-4f9617e91190
We're reading for Conditional, Causal, Mathematical, or Pivot wording, and we see a lot of Causal wording here. We might want to be assembling a Causal Chain
  *In a recession*
   *less consumer spending (******causes******)*
       *many businesses lay off or close down*
             *workers can't find new job (******resulting***** *in)*
                    *increased number of jobless people*
  Meanwhile, what gets us out of the recession?
  *more consumer spending*
       *expanded business activity (******creates******)*
           *new need for more workers*
               *recovery from recession has begun*
  Finally, in the last sentence, we have a pivot, **But**.
  Most Supported paragraphs frequently provide correct answers that **Reconcile the Pivot**; i.e. the correct answer safely synthesizes the tension the Pivot idea creates with what came before.
  We learn that businesses usually are too scared of a post-recession economy and delay hiring additional workers as long as possible.

##### 7ab616f7-4ee0-464e-b472-5168d64f8be3
Winnie's statement that "some bees navigate by doing X or by doing Y" has nothing to do with the honeybees' dance.

In fact, it's not even a statement about honeybees. It's implied that she's talking about other species of bees. They are not dancing; they are not sending friends to food sources. These other bees are just trying to navigate around the world.

This answer seems to imply that Winnie is bringing up these other bees in order to suggest that "the REAL explanation for the honeybees' dance is that ... *they use the dance to* *****navigate******"?*

That's definitely not what she's saying.

##### 9edd2aff-24ed-41c3-bba8-a8006e303da3
To prove not-just, we only need to establish that the 1st or the 2nd thing is not happening. This answer establishes that society S does **not** give equal right to basic liberties.

There are *****inequalities***** ** in the right to basic liberties.

##### feaa6a76-6633-4b36-8854-be08ec192ed0
This gives the us the typical "If *Evidence*, then *Conclusion*" answer we expect out of Strengthen-Principle.

** *****If***** *an activity is funded*      *****Then***** *funding*
   *by elected reps who  →  of that particular*
  *legitimately fund that        activity is warranted*
     *activity in general*

The activity we're talking about is "a work of art". Is it weird to call that an *activity*? Yeah, a bit. Technically, "****a work**** of art" does imply action. In a more common sense view, we know that artists are subsidized by a patron or grant money *while* they are working on a given work. So it's not like the government pays an artist for a finished product. The government pays the artist *to create* a finished product.

Okay, so the first sentence told us that elected reps are "within their rights to support the arts" (they legitimately fund the activity of art in general).

According to this principle, then, the funding of any particular work of art is warranted. *Warranted =* proper, fair, justified.

=== PT130-S1-Q13 | Main Conclusion | key B
STIM: It is a given that to be an intriguing person, one must be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others. Constantly broadening one's abilities and extending one's intellectual reach will enable one to inspire that curiosity. For such a perpetual expansion of one's mind makes it impossible to be fully comprehended, making one a constant mystery to others.
STEM: Which one of the following most accurately expresses the conclusion drawn in the argument above?
  A: To be an intriguing person, one must be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others.  [Problem]
  B: If one constantly broadens one's abilities and extends one's intellectual reach, one will be able to inspire the perpetual curiosity of others.  [Correct]
  C: If one's mind becomes impossible to fully comprehend, one will always be a mystery to others.  [Premise]
  D: To inspire the perpetual curiosity of others, one must constantly broaden one's abilities and extend one's intellectual reach.  [Reversal]
  E: If one constantly broadens one's abilities and extends one's intellectual reach, one will always have curiosity.  [Unsupported]
BANK KEY B

#### febd886e-8bc6-4f46-8bb0-9363231f470d
The author is thinking purely in terms of numbers.
She thinks that during the 17th century, major artists would have produced way fewer Dutch landscapes than minor artists would have (since minor artists were cranking these out prolifically, trying to satisfy the insatiable demands of the market).
So let's say for example that major artists created 100 of these paintings, whereas minor artists created 1000 of them.
The author is thinking, "if the surviving paintings are supposedly 1/2 major, 1/2 minor that's gotta be wrong. After all, the ratio of major to minor wasn't 1:1, it was more like 1 : 10."
We could call this aCurious Comparison-- how could it be that major artists made less than half of the Dutch landscapes in the 17th century but are attributed to half of the surviving ones?
The author's way of explaining this is, "We must be misattributing a bunch of these ones done by minor artists to major artists".
We, of course, are tasked with considering Alternate Explanations .... "Maybe a Dutch landscape painted by amajorartist would be much more likely to survive for several centuries than would one painted by a minor artist."
After all, the ones painted by major artists would be more valuable, presumably, so people would have more incentive to hang onto them. And perhaps they were owned by wealthier people, whose belongings and estates have been better preserved and attended to.
The Dutch landscapes painted by minor artists to satisfy rabid public demand might have been cheap to purchase, painted on cheaper (more degrading) materials, and thus many more of them would have ended up in the garbage eventually.

STIM: Critic: A novel cannot be of the highest quality unless most readers become emotionally engaged with the imaginary world it describes. Thus shifts of narrative point of view within a novel, either between first and third person or of some other sort, detract from the merit of the work, since such shifts tend to make most readers focus on the author.

  C: Most readers cannot become emotionally involved with a novel's imaginary world if they focus on the author.  [Correct]

STIM: Engine noise from boats traveling through killer whales’ habitats ranges in frequency from 100 hertz to 3,000 hertz, an acoustical range that overlaps that in which the whales communicate through screams and squeals. Though killer whales do not seem to behave differently around running boat engines, engine noise from boats can be loud enough to damage their hearing over time. Therefore, _______ .
STEM: Which one of the following most logically completes the argument?
  A: younger killer whales are better able to tolerate engine noise from boats than older whales are  [Contradiction]
  B: killer whales are less likely to attempt to communicate with one another when boat engines are operating nearby  [Out of Scope]
  C: noise from boat engines may impair killer whales’ ability to communicate  [Correct]
  D: killer whales are most likely to prefer areas where boat traffic is present, but light  [Out of Scope]
