=== PT158-S4-Q3 | Role | key C
STIM: Physician: We are constantly bombarded by warnings, based on initial studies' tentative conclusions, about this or that food having adverse health effects. If the medical establishment wants people to pay attention to health warnings, it should announce only conclusive results, the kind that can come only from definitive studies. After all, people who are constantly subjected to fire drills eventually come to ignore the fire alarm.
STEM: The statement that people who are constantly subjected to fire drills eventually come to ignore the fire alarm plays which one of the following roles in the physician's argument?
  A: It is presented as an example of the sort of warning referred to in the argument's overall conclusion.  [Not an Example of the Warnings]
  B: It is a statement that plays no logical role in the argument but that instead serves to impugn the motives of the medical establishment.  [Plays No Logical Role — Plainly Wrong]
  C: It is an analogy offered in support of the argument's overall conclusion.  [Correct]
  D: It is an analogy that forms part of a specific objection to the argument's overall conclusion.  [Direction Reversed]
  E: It is an analogy offered to clarify the distinction the physician makes between an initial study and a definitive study.  [Wrong Purpose]
  BANK A: It is presented as an example of the sort of warning referred to in the argument’s overall conclusion.
  BANK C: It is an analogy offered in support of the argument’s overall conclusion.
  BANK D: It is an analogy that forms part of a specific objection to the argument’s overall conclusion.
BANK KEY C
--- style standard a8407c96-3ae3-493a-a164-5d5119abee2c
  STEP 0 [Conclusion (should)] ee9a7112-d306-48a4-81fd-864a2707ecd8
If the medical establishment wants people to pay attention to health warnings, it should announce only conclusive results from definitive studies.
  STEP 1 [Evidence (after all)] 1c4d564c-dda6-43b5-955d-d0b5925a95ef
We're constantly bombarded by warnings about certain foods having adverse effects, based on initial studies' tentative.
People who are constantly subjected to fire drills eventually come to ignore the fire alarm.
  STEP 2 [Evaluate] 00e40578-bc91-4a71-8b9f-67df69fbcf56
The main conclusion should be an Author's Opinion and it should be supported by at least one claim. We can find our conclusion here by noticing the Opinion Indicator should in the 2nd sentence, and by noticing the Support Indicator after all beginning the 3rd sentence.
"After all" is the "A" in FABS: for, after all, because, since
Whenever we see "after all", we know the previous claim was a conclusion.
  STEP 3 [Goal] 88d2713b-fc3b-4661-bf77-db6938b0e762
We're being asked about the last claim, which we know was Support (the "after all" guarantees that). And we know the conclusion was the 2nd sentence.
  NOTE A 8661f25d-6939-4072-9f3f-5c6a8c8de525
This answer treats the fire alarm as a literal instance of the type of warning the physician is concerned about — as if the physician were compiling a list of warnings that cause desensitization. But the physician is not cataloging types of warnings. The fire alarm is brought in from a completely different domain (building safety, not health research) to serve as an analogy. Its function is to illustrate a principle — that repeated alerts lead to desensitization — not to exemplify the specific health warnings under discussion. An example would be: "People hear about a different food causing cancer every week." An analogy is: "It is like fire drills — repeat them enough and people stop reacting." The physician is doing the latter, using a familiar non-health scenario to illuminate the dynamics of the health-warning problem.
  NOTE B 3fe81613-9747-414f-88e2-79590bfbef3c
Claiming a statement "plays no logical role" means it is tangential, decorative, or irrelevant to the argument's structure. That description is plainly false here. The fire alarm analogy is one of the physician's primary tools for supporting the conclusion. It is introduced with "After all," which is a premise indicator signaling that what follows provides a reason to believe what came before. Remove the analogy and the argument loses a key piece of its persuasive architecture. Additionally, this answer suggests the statement is meant to impugn the motives of researchers, but the physician is not questioning researchers' motives at all — the physician is arguing about the practical consequences of how research findings are communicated. The fire alarm analogy addresses desensitization effects, not researchers' intentions.
  NOTE C 69ddbb44-f757-4cca-a093-b30747ae1d5c
The fire alarm statement functions as an analogy that supports the physician's overall conclusion. The physician's main claim is that researchers should announce only conclusive results because excessive warnings cause desensitization. The fire alarm reference illustrates this desensitization principle through a parallel from everyday life: people subjected to repeated fire drills eventually ignore the alarm, just as people bombarded with constant health warnings eventually tune them out. The word "After all" explicitly marks this as a premise — evidence offered in support of what preceded it. The analogy does not form the conclusion itself; it supports the conclusion by providing a familiar, intuitive case that demonstrates the underlying mechanism (repeated alerts leading to diminished response). Its role is to make the abstract principle of desensitization concrete and persuasive.
  NOTE D 58b22f57-d877-4108-be7e-8bd645d05f65
This answer gets the relationship between the analogy and the conclusion exactly backwards. An analogy that "forms part of an objection to the conclusion" would be deployed by an opponent to undermine the physician's reasoning. But the fire alarm analogy comes from the physician and is offered in direct support of the physician's own conclusion. The relationship between the analogy and the conclusion is cooperative, not adversarial. The physician introduces the fire alarm to make the case for limiting announcements stronger, not to raise doubts about it. If the analogy were being used against the physician's position, it would need to highlight why desensitization does not occur or why frequent warnings are beneficial despite their frequency. Instead, it does precisely the opposite.
  NOTE E 2c38e082-ad21-4901-bfb5-5482a206f718
Clarifying a distinction means separating two concepts that might be confused — demonstrating how they differ from each other. The fire alarm analogy does the opposite: it highlights a similarity between two scenarios, not a difference. The physician's rhetorical strategy depends on the audience seeing fire alarm desensitization and health warning desensitization as parallel phenomena, not as distinct ones. Furthermore, this answer specifies a distinction between "initial findings" and "definitive study results," but the fire alarm analogy does not map onto that distinction. The analogy illustrates the consequence of overexposure to warnings in general, not the difference between preliminary and conclusive research. The analogy's function is comparison, not differentiation.

Same principle applies to health warnings: warn people about everything and they end up ignoring everything, including the warnings that actually matter.
  STEP 2 [Evaluate] 8a425a1f-832c-4dd4-85dc-21f5df196322
The question asks what job the fire alarm statement does in the argument. Think of the argument like a building: the conclusion is the roof, and the fire alarm analogy is a support beam. It is not the roof itself (not the conclusion), not decorative wallpaper (not irrelevant background), and not a wrecking ball (not an objection). It is structural support — a premise in analogy form that holds up the main point by showing how desensitization works in a familiar context.
  NOTE A d941be9e-7f76-49b6-b34c-0b040b778e31
Hearing someone say "life is like a box of chocolates" and thinking they are literally recommending you buy chocolate — that is the mistake this answer makes. The fire alarm is not on the physician's "Top 10 Warnings People Should Stop Ignoring" list. It is an analogy brought in from a completely different world (building safety) to illustrate why health warning overload is a problem. Examples illustrate a category from within; analogies illuminate a principle from outside. The fire alarm is doing the second job.
  NOTE B f2669aba-4bd2-43f7-a927-0f7d675f6468
Saying the fire alarm analogy "plays no role" is like saying the engine plays no role in making a car move. This analogy is the workhorse of the argument — it is the mechanism through which the physician explains why warning overload is a problem. Take it out and the argument goes from a supported claim to a bald assertion. And "impugns motives"? The physician is not calling researchers shady — they are saying the communication strategy backfires. Two very different criticisms.
  NOTE C e9f86be2-3ff4-4e8a-902c-9ddd05aac238
The physician is pulling the oldest trick in the rhetoric book: "You know how everyone agrees about THIS? Well, my point is basically the same thing." Fire drills happen too often and people stop evacuating — obvious, right? The physician takes that universal agreement and maps it onto health warnings: warn people about everything and they stop listening to anything. It is an analogy doing premise duty, and "After all" is the name tag that says "Hi, I am evidence." The fire alarm supports the conclusion; it is not the conclusion itself. Supporting act, not headliner.
  NOTE D 794cd2d2-03e9-4a35-bd15-718519520e5d
The fire alarm analogy and the physician's conclusion are best friends, not enemies. This answer claims the analogy is objecting to the conclusion, but the analogy is cheering for it. It is the courtroom equivalent of calling the defense's own star witness a hostile witness for the prosecution. The physician brought the fire alarm to the party to help make the case, not to sabotage it. There is zero adversarial energy here — the analogy is all-in on team conclusion.
  NOTE E a594fde5-a8f3-4a20-8ab4-669cddd67d30
The fire alarm analogy is playing matchmaker: "Look how similar these two situations are!" It is emphatically not playing referee: "Let me explain how these two things are totally different." The physician says desensitization from health warnings works like desensitization from fire drills — that is a comparison, drawing things together. This answer describes the opposite function: pulling things apart to show a distinction. The analogy unites; it does not divide. Wrong job description entirely.

#### PT132-S4-Q18 steps single Evaluate 736d3c1e-8383-4f9b-ba53-324cb3c76fe2
There's a lot of symbol matching and conditional logic here, so we might go down the road of diagramming (watch the class recording, via the ▶︎ button on the highlighter toolbar, if you'd like to see a diagramming discussion). But essentially the argument boils down to this missing link: if we can't send spacecraft there, then we'll need these aliens to be capable of communicating with us. If we translate the "unless" in the conclusion into "if-not" form, the conclusion is saying, "If none of the aliens are as smart as us, then we won't be able to discover them anytime soon". Why? Because, "If none of the aliens are as smart as us, then they won't be capable of communicating with us". We're trying to line up the Conclusion's verbiage with that of the Evidence so that it's clearer what's being assumed. If I say, "If Sheila doesn't come, we won't have beer. Thus, If Shelia doesn't come, this party will suck.", then I'm assuming "If we don't have beer, this party will suck". This argument is saying, "If they aren't as smart, they won't be able to communicate with us. Thus, if they aren't as smart, we won't be able to determine their existence." So it's assuming, "If they can't communicate with us, then we won't be able to determine their existence". We might skeptically attack that assumption by thinking, "Wait — that's the only way for us to determine their existence? They have to communicate with us? Couldn't there be some other way to determine their existence?" The author would say, "No, sadly. I thought of that too, but there's no way for us to send spacecraft there." We would then reply, "the only way for us to determine their existence is to send spacecraft there or have them communicate with us? Why are those the only two ways? Couldn't we detect their presence through some sort of bio-marker we can observe via spectral analysis? If they're communicating with each other via radio waves, couldn't we potentially pick up on their signal with a radio telescope? They might not be smart enough to send a signal to us, but we might be smart enough to detect signals they're sending to each other."

#### PT140-S3-Q7 steps single Evidence 24ea4e12-02f0-41d8-82dd-eb659ee09260
This is an Anti-Causal conclusion, because the author is saying that these safety features are a waste of money (implying "they don't do anything / they might even make the situation worse"). Since our job here is to object to the author's conclusion, we have to try to argue that "These safety features are not a waste. They are improving safety." The author will retaliate by saying, "Then how come we see more pedestrian injuries occur at crosswalks with these features than they do at crosswalks without these features"? We would have to Make an Excuse for that evidence, as we often do on Anti-Causal arguments. Given our view that ... the stripes and flashing lights do increase safety at crosswalks, How can we explain why ... more pedestrian injuries occur at crosswalks marked in such a way? Maybe those crosswalks were already terrible before the stripes and flashing lights arrived. Perhaps the flashing lights and stripes are an attempt to deal with a terrible intersection. They make the situation better, but those crosswalks are still the worst. It would be like if China or the U.S. took some steps to curb greenhouse emissions. Their measures might reduce greenhouse emissions but China and the U.S. would still be the worst polluters in the world, because they're so much worse than everyone else. LSAT has used this type of argument a bunch (click on the bold if you'd like to see other examples): "The areas with the most cops have the most crime. Clearly, cops don't help stop crime". "The roads with bike lanes have the most incidents of bikes getting hit by cars. Clearly, bike lanes don't help increase safety". Our objections: - We sent the cops there because the area was so bad in terms of crime. - We added the bike lanes because there were so many bikes getting hit by cars.

#### PT158-S4-Q25 steps standard Goal f38237d6-a747-4bb6-8a15-72901fcc9ff8
Since we're trying to prove that some ppl in the Hanson Building are programmers, and we know that a bunch of people in BUN 17 are programmers, we want an answer to put BUN 17 into the Hanson Building.
note: the above is what a normal person would be thinking going to these answer choices. the below is for LSAT Supernerds
When we see Quantifier words like "most" and "some", it triggers our awareness of a certain LSAT game where we use different quantifiers to prove there's some overlap.
for example,
```
Some A's are B
+ All B's are C
=================
Some A's are C
```
The vast majority of Trait Overlap inferences use conditional logic (like "All B are C"), but there's one special inference that doesn't — the most + most.
```
Most A's are B
+ Most A's are C
=================
Some B's are C
```
Since this argument is providing a MOST premise and trying to conclude a SOME idea, we would suspect that we're missing the other MOST idea.
```
Most BUN 17 are programmers      prem
+ Most BUN 17 are Hanson          answer
=================
Some Hanson are programmers      conc
```
p.s. if you want to try another problem just like this, try this one.

#### PT102-S2-Q20 choic single D f5d5f711-bb09-4539-91e5-e5d3dbe5408e
This answer is also structured like this:
*That X is true does not imply that Y is true*

So we want to know, does the evidence match X and the conclusion match Y?

Did the evidence say "it's impossible to know the complete state of the universe at any given time?"

Yes, that's the Intermediate Conclusion..

Did the author use that to support a Conclusion that says or assumes, "there is no complete state of the universe at a given time".

Sigh. Yes, kind of?  Determinism is based on the idea that the complete state of the universe at moment X determines the complete state of the universe at moment Y.

The author is trying to refute this idea by saying, "How could that be true? After all, it's impossible for us to know the complete state of the universe at moment X"

And this answer is saying, "Sure, it's impossible for us to know the complete state, but there still *is* a complete state. Our lack of knowing it doesn't prove it doesn't exist. So determinism could still be correct."

This answer is written in a frustrating style that is pretty hard to hear as correct if you haven't already thought through your objection to the argument. If you want to see a similar example, try this one.

#### PT110-S3-Q15 choic single C ac446ec4-e827-4b4a-b1e1-709f0d61cc34
As we analyzed before, Susan never talks about contemporary art overall, whether it's become big business or not. In order to pick this, we'd have to derive from Susan's paragraph that she believes "contemporary art hasn't become big business".

The idea that one can still find some fresh / creative contemporary art at most small galleries is not strong support for the claim that "contemporary art hasn't become big business".

#### PT139-S1-Q5 choic single C cc6f411f-4064-4c6e-aab6-90ca6a633411
This presents an Alternate Explanation for why the decaf drinkers were twice as likely as the regular coffee drinker to develop arthritis:
it's not (as the author is concluding) that decaf contains something bad that increases your likelihood of getting arthritis, it's that regular coffee contains something good (caffeine, which is not in decaf) that decreases your likelihood of getting arthritis.
This is a tricky alternate explanation, because the coffee is still the causal reason for the difference in arthritis risk, but saying that "caffeinated coffee has an ingredient that helps to discourage arthritis" is a different explanation than "decaf coffee has an ingredient that helps to encourage arthritis.

#### PT140-S3-Q18 choic single D 307341de-0de9-41d8-9576-18f5a0bca99e
This calls out the Sampling flaw. The argument definitely relies on a sample (the recent survey of businesses that have purchased videoconferencing equipment).
Why is it reasonable to suppose that people who have already purchased videoconferencing equipment are unrepresentative of most companies?
It's the idea of a self-selecting sample. If we asked people who bought an iPhone rather than a Samsung, "do you think you chose well?", we're naturally going to get a majority of people saying Yes.
But that's because we're talking to people who thought about the tradeoffs between the two phones and decided that for them it made more sense to go with the iPhone. That doesn't mean that for other people the same would apply.
Maybe a cleaner example would be to ask people who graduated from dental school whether they are happy with their decision. If 85% of them say yes, would we conclude, "Oh, then most people would be happy to go through dental school"?
Of course not. Most of us don't want to be dentists, but the ones who do go to dental school and are by and large pleased with their choice.
Similarly, most businesses don't need videoconferencing equipment (I'm looking at you, Plumbers), but the minority of businesses who do will buy it and find it worth the cost.
If you want to try a somewhat similar problem, try this one.

#### PT155-S4-Q15 choic single D 35943917-419a-43ec-b3f3-1a6453c239d6
Whoa, "surmises"? That's a new one. Welcome to Test 89, everyone.

Surmises = takes for granted = presumes = assumes.

Did the author assume from a correlation that one factor caused another? Yes, the author assumes that because there was an association between winning the majority of NE voters and being a candidate who favors property tax reform, that "being a candidate who favors property tax reform caused these candidates to win a majority of NE voters".

This is the famous Causal flaw.
